# SOURCES — where revenger learns

Contract: fleet `.ufo/waves/kaika/SOURCES-CONTRACT-v1.md` (frozen 2026-10-19).
Admission of claims stays in `QUALITY.md`; this file records where they come
from, how far to trust each for which scope, and when to look again. Rows are
append-only: only `status`, `recheck` and `used-by` change in place, each change
logged old→new in the round lesson. Every row below was opened on its `opened`
date by the round that wrote it; access dates and ≤25-word quotes are in
`lessons/round-1.md` `## Gate — kaika continuation`.

| S110 | SoK: All You Ever Wanted to Know About x86/x64 Binary Disassembly But Were Afraid to Ask (Pang et al., IEEE S&P 2021) — https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.14266 (v1, 2020-07-28) | paper | A | The authors' own abstract claims about nine open-source disassemblers studied over 3,788 binaries, as published in arXiv v1. | Abstract, arXiv:2007.14266v1 (2020-07-28), accessed 2026-10-29 — "We find that while principled algorithms are used by all tools, they still heavily rely on heuristics to increase code coverage." | Every disassembly result mixes algorithmic recovery with heuristic recovery. The ledger should record which disassembler and which heuristic produced an instruction or function boundary, and should not treat disassembly as a single fact. Limit: Rankings cover specific 2020-era tool versions and x86/x64 only; do not rank today's tools from them. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S111 | Ground Truth for Binary Disassembly is Not Easy (Pang, Zhang, Yu, Mao, Xu; USENIX Security 2022, pp. 2479–2495) — https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/pang-chengbin | paper | A | The authors' taxonomy of five ground-truth-collection mechanisms, their recommendation of compile-process tracing, and their extension of that approach to ARM32/AArch64/MIPS. | Abstract on USENIX presentation page (Sec '22, artifact badges: available/functional/reproduced), accessed 2026-10-29 — "The mechanism best serving today's need is to trace the compiling process of the target binaries to collect the ground truth information." | A byte-oracle fixture's ground truth is only as good as how it was collected. Derive it from the build (compiler/linker tracing), not from another disassembler or from debug symbols alone, and record the collection mechanism with the fixture. Limit: Covers compiler-built binaries where you control the build; it gives no ground-truth method for shipped third-party artifacts. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S112 | An In-Depth Analysis of Disassembly on Full-Scale x86/x64 Binaries (Andriesse, Chen, van der Veen, Slowinska, Bos; USENIX Security 2016, pp. 583–600) — https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity16/technical-sessions/presentation/andriesse | paper | A | The authors' abstract claims from evaluating nine disassemblers on 981 compiler-generated binaries, plus their review of 30 security papers. | Abstract on USENIX presentation page (Sec '16), accessed 2026-10-29 — "some constructs, such as function boundaries, are much harder to recover accurately than is reflected in the literature" | Function-boundary claims deserve lower default confidence than instruction-level claims. Treat recovered function starts and ends as inference unless a symbol table, unwind data or build trace corroborates them. Limit: Covers compiler-generated, non-obfuscated binaries; it supports no conclusion about packed or hand-written code. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S113 | Ahoy SAILR! There is No Need to DREAM of C: A Compiler-Aware Structuring Algorithm for Binary Decompilation (Basque et al., USENIX Security 2024, pp. 361–378) — https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/basque | paper | A | The authors' claims that spurious gotos come from compiler optimizations, plus their open-source angr decompiler implementations of SAILR, Phoenix, DREAM and Combing. | Abstract on USENIX page (Sec '24, artifact available/functional), accessed 2026-10-29 — "the root cause of spurious gotos is compiler-induced optimizations that occur at all optimization levels (17% in non-optimized compilation)" | Do not log the control-flow shape of decompiled output as the source's shape. Structuring artifacts (gotos, duplicated blocks) can come from compiler transforms, and closeness to the source is a separate, measurable property. Limit: The evaluation centers on GCC-style transforms; it does not establish source-structure fidelity for other compilers or obfuscated code. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S114 | No More Gotos: Decompilation Using Pattern-Independent Control-Flow Structuring and Semantics-Preserving Transformations (Yakdan, Eschweiler, Gerhards-Padilla, Smith; NDSS 2015) — https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss2015/ndss-2015-programme/no-more-gotos-decompilation-using-pattern-independent-control-flow-structuring-and-semantics/ | paper | A | The authors' description of DREAM's goto-free structuring and its coreutils comparison against Hex-Rays and Phoenix. | Abstract, NDSS 2015 programme page (dated 7 Feb 2015; page modified 2023-07-25), accessed 2026-10-29 — "Whenever no match is found they generate goto statements and thus produce unstructured decompiled output." | A goto in decompiler output can mean "the structurer found no pattern", not "the binary contains a goto". Log it as a tool limitation, not as a behavioral fact. Limit: Its goal of zero gotos is a readability target, and SAILR (above) disputes it as a fidelity target; do not read "goto-free" as "correct". | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S115 | Native x86 Decompilation Using Semantics-Preserving Structural Analysis and Iterative Control-Flow Structuring (Schwartz, Lee, Woo, Brumley; USENIX Security 2013, pp. 353–368) — https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity13/technical-sessions/presentation/schwartz | paper | A | The authors' claim that earlier structuring algorithms were not semantics-preserving, and their Phoenix evaluation on 107 coreutils programs. | Abstract on USENIX Sec '13 page, accessed 2026-10-29 — "existing structuring algorithms are not semantics-preserving, which means that they cannot safely be used for decompilation without modification" [integrator re-opened and located the quote] | Structuring is a transformation that can change meaning. A decompiler's C is a derived artifact whose correctness needs its own check, separate from the disassembly it came from. Limit: The "outperforms Hex-Rays by 114%" claim is about 2013 versions under the authors' own correctness metric; it is not evidence about current Hex-Rays. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

Type recovery and optimized code are where decompiler C is least trustworthy, so claims built on recovered types need corroboration. Limit: Error-triggering inputs are not distinct bugs, and results describe 2020 versions (IDA, JEB3, RetDec, r2ghidra), not current tools. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

A ledger should mark such results "attested, not reproducible" and keep them apart from the reproducible RetDec/r2ghidra results. Limit: Pins old dependencies (Ubuntu 18.04, RetDec v4.0, a 2020 radare2 commit); do not assume it runs today. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S118 | Scalable Validation of Binary Lifters (Dasgupta, Dinesh, Venkatesh, Adve, Fletcher; PLDI 2020) — implementation README at https://github.com/sdasgup3/validating-binary-decompilation (@ c0fcd6f099e38195dcbbac9e8c13a825865c5cb5) | code | A | The repo's own description of single-instruction validation (K-framework x86-64 semantics vs LLVM symbolic summaries, checked with Z3) and program-level validation (graph matching), plus its claim of 29 McSema bugs. | README intro, commit c0fcd6f, accessed 2026-10-29 — "if these summaries are not logically equivalent, a bug is detected in McSema." | Lifted IR can be checked per instruction against an independent formal ISA semantics. Treat a lifter's IR as a translation that needs validation before facts derived from it are cited. Limit: Covers McSema/Remill x86-64 → LLVM at that time only; it does not certify any other lifter or later versions. The PLDI paper PDF itself was not opened. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

LLM-cleaned decompiler output can silently drop the very guard or crash under investigation, so keep the raw decompiler placeholders as evidence and treat any rewrite as inference. Limit: Preprint with no peer-review status; figures apply to the systems and corpora it tested. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

A ledger should never use "reads cleanly" as evidence of correctness. Limit: The understandability scores come from an LLM judge, not humans; do not cite them as analyst-validated. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

A ledger entry citing a benchmark score should record which metrics actually ran, and a missing input should block the claim rather than shrink it. Limit: Self-described as "experimental" and "living", so scores change over time; pin the snapshot before citing any number. The leaderboard site itself was not opened. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

Limit: Describes Remill's design only; it is no evidence that any particular instruction's semantics are correct (see the validation rows above). | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

[province fit: adjacent] | README § intro, commit c655710, accessed 2026-10-29 — "see how that code looks after being compiled in real time." Its asm view is filtered, so it is not a byte-exact image of a linked binary. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

[province fit: adjacent] | Abstract, arXiv:2609.16423v1 (2026-09-14), accessed 2026-10-29 — "we treat every byte offset as a potential branch target and lift the entire binary in a brute-force manner" | Statically recovering a CFG is impossible in general, so a superset (every offset) is the only heuristic-free over-approximation. A ledger's code/data split is an inference that should name the heuristic used. Limit: Preprint and prototype; the abstract reports no scale or overhead figures, and recompilation is outside province (cite only the disassembly lesson). | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

NOT the calling convention (that is low-level-sys-info.tex, unopened). | chapter "Object Files", subsection "EH_FRAME sections", tables "Common Information Entry (CIE)", "CIE Augmentation Section Content", "Frame Descriptor Entry (FDE)"; repo x86-psABIs/x86-64-ABI HEAD e1ce098331da5dbd66e1ffc74162380bcc213236 (2025-03-12); object-files.tex last changed e178c71028bb13da1464273ec51efcf6ddd661f5 (2024-11-16). "If the code for a function is not one contiguous block, there will be a separate FDE for each contiguous sub-piece." | parse `.eh_frame` byte-for-byte (4-byte length, CIE id 0, augmentation "zR"/"zPLR", pointer encodings) and cross-check that cold/split function parts each carry their own FDE — a function-boundary oracle independent of symbols or disassembly. Limit: it is TeX source (not rendered); x86-64 only; `.eh_frame_hdr` is delegated to LSB ch.7 ("enhanced LSB Chapter 7"), not reproduced here. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

An agent can state which of several declared headers actually governed loading, and flag when a static reader picked a different duplicate. Limit: Covers only three header types; says nothing on PT_NOTE, PT_LOAD overlap or section headers; `next` tree may change. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

| S255 | Unicorn Engine FAQ — https://github.com/unicorn-engine/unicorn/blob/master/docs/FAQ.md | docs | A | Unicorn behavior caveats: PC sync, hooks, TB caching, MMU and TLB, MIPS kseg and ARM M-class non-executable regions. Version-scoped: the PC rules differ before and after 2.1.4. | docs/FAQ.md "Why do I get a wrong PC after emulation stops?" (sections "For version < 2.1.4" and ">= 2.1.4"), "Editing an instruction doesn't take effect"; `master` @ `7c5db94191de` (2026-02-17); latest release `2.1.4` (2025-09-09); GPLv2. "We should always have valid PC all the time. Please report an issue for your case." (FAQ, "For version >= 2.1.4" | An agent can interpret emulation outcomes correctly. Before 2.1.4 the PC may be left at the start of the basic block on an exception unless code/memory hooks are installed. TB chaining means patched code needs `uc_ctl_remove_cache`. ARM THUMB needs `UC_MODE_THUMB` with an odd start address, and `uc_mem_map` handles physical addresses. Limit: Unicorn is a CPU-only emulator, so there are no OS or syscall semantics. It "does lots of dirty hacks within qemu code" and bases on qemu 5.0.1 since 2.0.0, so newer instructions may be unsupported. The FAQ is maintainer prose and does not cover every ISA quirk. The README banner says "LOOKING FOR CONTRIBUTORS!", so maintenance cadence is a concern. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

Limit: README states "Rizin has a quickly evolving C API", so rz-ghidra versions are paired with Rizin versions and release numbers are "not guaranteed" to match. | S349 | MITRE ATT&CK — "Obfuscated Files or Information, Technique T1027" v2.0 (ATT&CK v20) — https://attack.mitre.org/versions/v20/techniques/T1027/ | spec | A | ATT&CK's own definition of T1027 and its 18 sub-technique names (e.g., T1027.002 Software Packing, .007 Dynamic API Resolution, .016 Junk Code Insertion) as of v20. [province fit: adjacent] | T1027 page, "Version: 2.0", "Last Modified: 11 September 2026", tactic shown as "Stealth" (TA0005); accessed 2026-10-29; "Payloads may also be split into separate, seemingly benign files that only reveal malicious functionality when reassembled." | Use the stable sub-technique IDs as labels for an observed anti-analysis trait. In v20 the tactic label for TA0005 appears as "Stealth", so labels must be pinned to an ATT&CK version. Limit: The procedure examples are vendor-report citations that ATT&CK did not verify. Do not use them as byte-level evidence of any family's behavior. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S350 | MITRE CAPEC — "CAPEC-188: Reverse Engineering" (CAPEC List Version 3.9) — https://capec.mitre.org/data/definitions/188.html | spec | A | CAPEC's meta-level definition of reverse engineering, its white-box/black-box children (CAPEC-167, CAPEC-189), and the listed mitigation. [province fit: adjacent] | "Example Instances" section; content history 2023-01-24 (Version 3.9); accessed 2026-10-29; "'Black Box' methods involve interacting with the software indirectly, in the absence of the ability to measure, instrument, or analyze an executable object directly." | CAPEC treats RE as an adversary attack pattern and lists obfuscation as its mitigation. That framing matters when explaining scope and refusals: our lane covers white-box artifact analysis only. Limit: This is a classification entry, not a method. "Likelihood Of Attack: Low" is MITRE's rating and is not an observation. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |
| S351 | Unprotect Project — evasion technique database (Roccia, Lesueur) — https://unprotect.it/about/ (index https://unprotect.it/) | community | C | The project's self-description and its counts at the time of access: 376 techniques with U-IDs, 242 snippets, 160 detection rules. [province fit: adjacent] | About page, "Platform Version: 7.0.7 / Last Update: 2026-03-30"; accessed 2026-10-29; "The goal of this free database is to centralize the information about malware evasion techniques." | A community catalog with per-technique U-IDs and YARA rules (e.g., "Detect Disassembly Obfuscation Rogue Byte") is a lead index for naming an anti-analysis trait. It does not confirm the trait. Limit: Entries are contributed and not peer-reviewed, and snippets are offensive PoCs. Cite only technique names and detection rules, never snippet code. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

[province fit: adjacent] | "Debug Flags" intro; article published 2024-07-10; accessed 2026-10-29; "The states of these flags can be verified either by using specific API functions or examining the system tables in memory." | Anti-debug checks show up statically as specific API imports or resolved names plus PEB/heap field offsets. That gives byte-checkable recognition anchors (e.g., ProcessInformationClass 0x1f) for a ledger. Limit: This is a vendor research catalog of Windows-only checks. The "Mitigations" sections are debugger-hiding advice, which is outside this lane's static scope. Do not cite them as procedure. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

[province fit: adjacent] | README "Introduction" and "Features" (Al-Khaser v0.81); accessed 2026-10-29; "It performs a bunch of common malware tricks with the goal of seeing if you stay under the radar." | An executable, named list of checks gives a cross-reference vocabulary for anti-analysis traits found during static triage, e.g., "Interrupt 0x2d", "PAGE_GUARD memory breakpoints". Limit: It is a PoC test binary for analysis labs. Running it or hardening a sandbox against it is outside the static, artifact-first province. Use the README as a name catalog only. | mined | 2026-10-29 | 90d | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

In a ledger it is an inference that needs an equivalence check before it becomes an observation. Limit: Its handler results on VMProtect/Themida are research measurements on 2017 versions. This row carries no claim about current protectors and no procedure for them. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

A synthesis failure is therefore an "unknown", not evidence that no simple semantics exists. Limit: The arXiv title differs from the CCS title, so cite both. The success rates are on the authors' benchmarks. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

Limit: The property applies to linear MBA. It is not a claim about polynomial or non-linear MBA, or about arbitrary bit-vector code. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

[province fit: adjacent] | Abstract, v3 (latest listed); accessed 2026-10-29; "Loki protects against previously unaccounted attack vectors such as program synthesis, for which it reduces the success rate to merely 19%." | Each deobfuscation method has a published countermeasure. Limit: The headline about simple models matching deep ones applies only to the paper's benchmark and features. | mined | 2026-10-29 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/round-73-re-resource-atlas.md |

## Paths

- `authorized bounty triage` — S050 → S051: the maintainer's written-use boundary and disclosure window first, then report-structure discipline as corroboration, with the lane's own Q01-Q16 and package validator carrying every rule.
- `scoring a tool claim` — S003 → S004 → S007 → S014: lab testing requirements organized by function, the CFTT methodology that instantiates them, where version-scoped reports live, then the lane's own r7/r8 scoring as the worked case.
- `what a digest proves` — S006 → S005 → S014: the digest's definition first, then acquisition-verification scope, then the lane's r35 reformat as the counter-example.
- `decoding a resource name field` — S009 → S010 → S011 → S013: where the name field lives in the PE spec, judging its bytes against Unicode definitions, reproducing decoder modes, then the saved parser run that accepted an ill-formed name.
- `host readiness` — S002 → S001: what the installed skill declares, then what the host actually has.
- `reading a saved EVM interface record` — S016 → S017 → S018 → S019 → S026: opcode values and the PUSH-data rule at a pinned tag, the paper's valid-destination definition, what a real selector is (and so why the fixtures' are constants), the metadata tail the fixtures lack, then the lane's seven saved outputs.
- `reading a saved CommonJS import edge` — S022 → S020 → S021 → S023 → S027: what destructuring evaluates, how Node resolves the relative specifier and the folder fallback, what `main` means, why export detection stays heuristic, then the two saved graphs.
- `round-62 byte oracle, not provider method` — S028 → S031: first fix opcode-boundary bookkeeping at a pinned specification revision, then compare authored carrier/decoded bytes and literal saved fields without inferring execution or completeness.
- `round-62 source names, not runtime imports` — S029 → S030: separate the language's object-binding and static-import roles before matching the fixture source names and call spans to literal saved graph fields.
- `C05 authored APK field readout` — S038 → S048 → S032/S034 → S041 → S047 → S049 → S052: AXML structures and enums, DEX declared header semantics, ZIP record layouts, the authored bytes/oracle, the anchor derivation, documented route scope, and finally the command's actual source-line output format. S053 is rejected as non-load-bearing for this manual-readout entry.
- `selected RE tool sources and trust limits` — S054–S070 + S073 for each primary project's own surface; S075–S080 for the REA setup/prompt/spec example and two learning courses; S081–S087 for decompilation/human-workflow research and first-party REA commentary; S088–S095 for scoped catalogs, publisher pages, a course guide, OWASP mobile guidance and the REA feature index. Catalog rows are leads, never standalone tool evidence.
- `evidence-ledger discipline (C10)` — S103 → S096 → S098 → S100 → S097 → S099 → S102 → S108 for REA Evidence v2/ledger/unknown/completion contracts; S101 for product non-goals; S104/S106/S107/S109 as adjacent accountability/custody/agentic-survey/cognitive-uncertainty method; S105 as tier-B black-box method lead only; S002 for the skill's finding-ledger rule.
- `is this disassembly or decompilation right?` — S110 → S111 → S112 → S116 → S117 → S373 → S115 → S113: first what disassembly and ground truth can and cannot establish, then correctness testing of decompilers, structuring semantics, and the fidelity taxonomy; a decompiler listing is an inference until a byte, spec or behavioral oracle supports it.
- `lifter or IR correctness` — S118 → S377 → S123 → S131 → S132: lifters and P-code semantics as claims to test against native execution, not as truth; open the validators before trusting lifted output.
- `naming and types recovered by models` — S361 → S360 → S362 → S363 → S122 → S120 → S364: learned names and types are suggestions scored on benchmarks; carry each as an inference with its benchmark scope.
- `byte-level format oracles` — S141 → S135 → S143 → S144 → S147 → S136 → S162 → S159 → S134: primary specifications to derive an expected value from, before quoting a tool.
- `what a parser really accepts` — S181 → S194 → S195 → S196 → S188 → S175 → S176 → S183: where real loaders and identifiers disagree with the specifications; use to decide which parser claim is a hypothesis.
- `describing a format as code` — S168 → S169 → S171 → S172 → S177 → S179: format-description languages as independent re-derivation tools for authored byte fixtures.
- `behavior and capability claims` — S205 → S206 → S207 → S209 → S210 → S219 → S248: rule hits, extracted strings and emulation traces as labelled inferences with stated blind spots.
- `patch and build diffing` — S223 → S224 → S225 → S226 → S227 → S404: a diff or match is a correspondence claim; record the matcher and its score and treat unmatched as unknown.
- `emulation, symbolic and time-travel evidence` — S213 → S212 → S216 → S217 → S242 → S229 → S230 → S231: execution-derived evidence is bounded by the emulated surface; read each project's own limitation text first.
- `framework-specific recovery (Go, Rust, Dart, Hermes, Python, JS bundles, .NET, JVM)` — S211 → S259 → S264 → S266 → S269 → S270 → S272 → S273 → S277 → S278 → S282 → S284 → S285: identify the packaging first, then pick the extractor whose own README names that packaging and version; recovered source is a reconstruction.
- `APK and Electron integrity` — S294 → S295 → S296 → S297 → S298 → S288 → S289: signature and integrity statements bound to the container layout; a valid signature says nothing about benign behavior.
- `firmware and boot images` — S306 → S308 → S307 → S251 → S252 → S322 → S323 → S324 → S325 → S326 → S331 → S330: carve and identify with two independent extractors, decode headers from the specs, and record unextracted regions as unknown.
- `what a hash, signature or reproducible build proves` — S334 → S335 → S336 → S337 → S338 → S339 → S340 → S341: provenance claims limited to the verified subject and signer; extends the lane's digest-scope entry.
- `obfuscation and packing vocabulary (recognition only)` — S343 → S344 → S345 → S348 → S349 → S350 → S351 → S354 → S356: taxonomies to name what the artifact shows; no row authorizes defeating a commercial protection.
- `artifact text as untrusted input to an RE agent` — S368 → S369 → S370 → S367 → S366: strings and comments inside samples may target the analyst model; extends S076 and S085.
- `instruction-set ground truth` — S379 → S380 → S381 → S383 → S384 → S382 → S387: vendor manuals decide what an instruction does; community tables are leads.
- `self-training corpora for RE judgment` — S386 → S390 → S391 → S388 → S389 → S403 → S392 → S398: practice material with checkable answers; run only on authored or explicitly permitted artifacts.
- `REA quick-start core (host contract, CLI map, evidence workflows)` — S415 → S416 → S417 → S418 → S419 → S420 → S421 → S422 → S423 → S424 → S425 → S426: host-local REA documents and obscure Ghidra P-code/corpus leads queued by the REA quick-start slice; status queued, no knowledge entry cites them yet.
